\underline{\bar{a}}
c
\underline{c}

A

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 3.0mu$}}

\parallel_{0}a.b
\parallel_{1}\overline{a}.c
\parallel_{0}\hat{a}.b
\parallel_{1}\hat{\overline{a}}.c
\parallel_{0}\hat{a}.\hat{b}
\parallel_{1}\hat{\bar{a}}.\hat{c}
\underline{a}
\underline{\overline{a}}
\underline{\overline{b}}
\underline{c}
\underline{\tau}
a
\underline{a}
b
\underline{b}
\tau
\tau
\bar{a}
\underline{\bar{a}}
c
\underline{c}

B

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 3.0mu$}}

Figure 15
.
Example of nets corresponding to RCCS process
R_{1}
and
R_{2}

We have that the path of the left process is

\mathtt{path}(\langle{*},{a^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle 0\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}})={\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}
, while
the path of the right process is
\mathtt{path}(\langle 1\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle})={\sf\|_{1}}
.

The encoding of an RCCS process should yield an equivalent net to that of its
ancestor, with the only potential distinction being the marking – indicating
the specific locations where tokens are placed.
And such positions are inferred from the information stored in memories.
Following the intuitions in
Section

4
, we will treat names of places
and transitions as strings. When we write
\phi X
, where
X
is a set of
strings and
\phi\in\{{\sf\|_{i}},{\sf+_{i}},\hat{\alpha},{\sf\setminus_{a}}\}
,
we are indicating the set
\{\phi x\,\mid\,x\in X\}
.
Also, we will indicate with
\tilde{\phi}
the sequence
\phi_{1}\cdots\phi_{n}

with
\phi_{i}\in\{{\sf\|_{j}},{\sf+_{j}},\hat{\alpha},{\sf\setminus_{a}}\}
.
Then the
marking
function
\mathbf{\mu}({\cdot})
is inductively defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R\parallel S})=\

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R})\bowtie\mathbf{\mu}({S})

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R\backslash a})=\

\displaystyle{\sf\setminus_{a}}\mathbf{\mu}({R})

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({m\cdot\langle m_{1},\alpha^{i},\mathbf{0}\rangle\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright P})=\

\displaystyle\{\alpha,m_{1}\}\cup\hat{\alpha}.\mathbf{\mu}({m\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright P})

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({m\cdot\langle m_{1},\alpha^{i},Q\rangle\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright P})=\

\displaystyle\{{\sf+_{i}}\alpha,m_{1}\}\cup{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\alpha}.\mathbf{\mu}({m\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright P})

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({m\cdot\langle*,\alpha^{i},\mathbf{0}\rangle\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright P})=\

\displaystyle\{\hat{\alpha}.\underline{\alpha}\}\cup\hat{\alpha}.\mathbf{\mu}({m\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright P})

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({m\cdot\langle*,\alpha^{i},Q\rangle\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright P})=\

\displaystyle\{{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\alpha}.\underline{\alpha}\}\cup{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\alpha}.\mathbf{\mu}({m\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright P})

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({m\cdot\langle i\rangle\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright P})=\

\displaystyle{\sf\|_{i}}\,\mathbf{\mu}({m\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright P})

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({\langle\rangle\triangleright P})=\

\displaystyle\{P\}

where
\bowtie
is defined as the usual set union on single element, and as the
merge on pairs of the form
\{t_{1},m_{2}\}

\{t_{2},m_{1}\}
where

\{t_{1},m_{2}\}\bowtie\{t_{2},m_{1}\}=s_{\{t_{1},t_{2}\}}
if

{\ell(t_{1})}=\overline{\ell(t_{2})}
and
t_{i}=\mathtt{path}(m_{i})\alpha_{i}
with
\alpha_{i}=\ell(t_{i})
,
where
s_{\{t_{1},t_{2}\}}
is the key place of the synchronisation between transitions

t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.

{exa}

Let us consider the RCCS processes
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
of Example
4.2
.
The marking of the process
R_{1}
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{a^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle 0\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}}\triangleright b\parallel\langle 1\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}\triangleright\bar{a}.c})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{a^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle 0\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}}\triangleright b}))\bowtie({\sf\|_{1}}\mathbf{\mu}({\langle\rangle\triangleright\bar{a}.c}))

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle({\sf\|_{0}}\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{a^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}\triangleright b}))\bowtie(\{{\sf\|_{1}}\bar{a}.c\})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle(\{{\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}.\underline{a}\}\cup{\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}.\mathbf{\mu}({\langle\rangle\triangleright b}))\bowtie\{{\sf\|_{1}}\bar{a}.c\}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\{{\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}.\underline{a},{\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}.b\}\bowtie\{{\sf\|_{1}}\bar{a}.c\}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\{{\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}.\underline{a},{\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}.b,{\sf\|_{1}}\bar{a}.c\}

and the marking of the process
R_{2}
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{b^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle{m_{2}},{a^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle 0\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}}}\triangleright\mathbf{0}\parallel\langle{m_{1}},{\overline{a}^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle 1\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}}\triangleright c})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle(\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{b^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle{m_{2}},{a^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle 0\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}}}\triangleright\mathbf{0}}))\bowtie(\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{m_{1}},{\overline{a}^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle 1\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}}\triangleright c}))

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle({\sf\|_{0}}\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{b^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle{m_{2}},{a^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}}\triangleright\mathbf{0}}))\bowtie({\sf\|_{1}}\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{m_{1}},{\overline{a}^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}\triangleright c}))

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle{\sf\|_{0}}(\{a,m_{2}\},\hat{a}.\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{b^{1}},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\langle\rangle}\triangleright\mathbf{0}}))\bowtie({\sf\|_{1}}\{\{\overline{a},m_{1}\},\hat{\overline{a}}.\mathbf{\mu}({{\langle\rangle}\triangleright c})\})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle{\sf\|_{0}}(\{a,m_{2}\},\hat{a}.\{\underline{b},b\})\bowtie({\sf\|_{1}}\{\{\overline{a},m_{1}\},\hat{\overline{a}}.c\})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\{\{{\sf\|_{0}}a,m_{2}\},{\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}.\underline{b},{\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}.\hat{b}\}\bowtie\{\{{\sf\|_{1}}\overline{a},m_{1}\},{\sf\|_{1}}\hat{\overline{a}}.c\}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\{\{{\sf\|_{0}}a,{\sf\|_{1}}\overline{a}\},{\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}.\underline{b},{\sf\|_{0}}\hat{a}.\hat{b},{\sf\|_{1}}\hat{\overline{a}}.c\}

We are now in place to define a property that relates the definitions of

\mathbf{\mu}({\cdot})
and
\mathtt{path}(\cdot)
with RCCS processes.

{pty}

Let
R=m\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}.P_{i}
be a RCCS process. For any
z\in I

such that
R\xrightarrow{{m}:{\alpha_{z}}}\langle{*},{\alpha^{z}_{z}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{z\}}\alpha_{i}.P_{i}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P_{z}
we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{\alpha^{z}_{z}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{z\}}\alpha_{i}.P_{i}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P_{z}})=\

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R})\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}

\displaystyle\cup\ \{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\underline{\alpha_{z}},\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}

Proof 4.3
.

The proof is by induction on the size of
m
. The base case with
m=\langle\rangle
trivially holds.
In the inductive case we have
m=m_{1}\cdot e\cdot\langle\rangle
where
e
can be

\langle i\rangle
,
\langle*,\beta^{i},Q\rangle
or
\langle m_{2},\beta^{i},Q\rangle
.
We will show the first two cases, with the third being similar to the second one.
We have that

S_{0}=m_{1}\cdot\langle\rangle\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}.P_{i}\xrightarrow{{m_{1}\cdot\langle\rangle}:{\alpha_{z}}}\langle{*},{\alpha^{z}_{z}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{z\}}\alpha_{i}.P_{i}}\rangle\cdot{m_{1}\cdot\langle\rangle}\triangleright P_{z}=R_{0}

and by applying inductive hypothesis (on a shorter memory) we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({S_{0}})=\mathbf{\mu}({R_{0}})\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\underline{\alpha_{z}},\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}

(1)

We proceed by case analysis.

e=\langle i\rangle
:

let us note that
\mathtt{path}(m)={\sf\|_{i}}\mathtt{path}(m_{1})
, and that

\mathbf{\mu}({R})={\sf\|_{i}}\mathbf{\mu}({R_{0}})
and
\mathbf{\mu}({S})={\sf\|_{i}}\mathbf{\mu}({S_{0}})
. Thanks to
Eq.

1
we know the form of
\mathbf{\mu}({S_{0}})
, hence

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({S})=\

\displaystyle{\sf\|_{i}}\mathbf{\mu}({S_{0}})

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle{\sf\|_{i}}\mathbf{\mu}({R_{0}})\setminus\{{\sf\|_{i}}\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}\cup\{{\sf\|_{i}}\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\underline{\alpha_{z}},{\sf\|_{i}}\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R})\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\underline{\alpha_{z}},\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}

as desired.

e=\langle*,\beta^{i},Q\rangle
:

let us note that

\mathtt{path}(m)={\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\mathtt{path}(m_{1})
, and that

\mathbf{\mu}({R})={\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\mathbf{\mu}({R_{0}})\cup\{{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\underline{\beta}\}
,
and
\mathbf{\mu}({S})={\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\mathbf{\mu}({S_{0}})\cup\{{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\underline{\beta}\}
.
Thanks to
Eq.

1
we know the form of
\mathbf{\mu}({S_{0}})
, hence

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({S})=\

\displaystyle{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\mathbf{\mu}({S_{0}})\cup\{{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\underline{\beta}\}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\{\mathbf{\mu}({R_{0}})\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}

\displaystyle\cup\ \{\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\underline{\alpha_{z}},\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}\}\ \cup\ \{{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\underline{\beta}\}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\mathbf{\mu}({R_{0}})\setminus\{{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\underline{\alpha_{z}},{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\mathtt{path}(m_{1}){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}\}\cup\{{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\underline{\beta}\}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\mathbf{\mu}({R_{0}})\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}

\displaystyle\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\underline{\alpha_{z}},\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}\}\cup\{{\sf+_{i}}\hat{\beta}.\underline{\beta}\}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R})\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\underline{\alpha_{z}},\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z}.P_{z}\}\}

as desired.

As a consequence, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 9
.

Let
R=m\triangleright\alpha.P
be a RCCS process. For any
z\in I

such that
R\xrightarrow{{m}:{\alpha_{z}}}\langle{*},{\alpha},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P
we have that

\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{\alpha},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P})=\mathbf{\mu}({R})\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha.P\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m)\hat{\alpha}.\underline{\alpha},\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha.P\}

We are now ready to formalise the reversible net corresponding to an RCCS process.

{defi}

Let
R
be an RCCS term with
{\rho}(R)=P
. Then

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R)\mkern 3.0mu$}}

is the net
\langle S,T,F,\mathbf{\mu}({R})\rangle
where

{\mathcal{N}(P)}=\langle S{},T{},F{},\mathsf{m}{}\rangle
.

Note that the reversible net corresponding to a coherent RCCS process
R

retains identical places, transitions, and flow relationships as the ancestor
of R. The sole divergence lies in the marking, which is derived through the
utilisation of the computational history stored within the memories of
R
.
The following Proposition is a consequence of the Proposition
7
,
Lemma
8
and of the definition of
\mathbf{\mu}({\cdot})
.

Proposition 10
.

Let
R
be an RCCS term with
{\rho}(R)=P
. Then

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R)\mkern 3.0mu$}}

is a reversible unravel net.

4.3.
Correctness result

We prove the correctness of our encoding in terms of a behavioural
equivalence. To this aim we reformulate the definition of
forward and
reverse bisimilarity

[
PU07
]
, initially stated for CCSK, to cope with
RCCS terms and Petri nets.

{defi}

[Forward and reverse bisimulation]
Let
R
a coherent RCCS process and
N=\langle S,T,F,\mathsf{m}\rangle
an

rUN
. The relation
\mathcal{R}
is a forward reverse bisimulation if
whenever
(R,N)\in\mathcal{R}
:

(1)

if
R\xrightarrow{{m}:{\alpha}}R^{\prime}
then there exist
t\in T
and

\mathsf{m^{\prime}}
such that
\mathsf{m}\,[\/{t}\/\rangle\,\mathsf{m^{\prime}}
,

t=(\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha,\mathtt{f})
and

(R^{\prime},\langle S,T,F,\mathsf{m}^{\prime}\rangle)\in\mathcal{R}
;

(2)

if
R\mathrel{\hbox to19.66pt{\vbox to10.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 9.83215pt\lower-6.45969pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

(3)

then there exist

(t_{1},\mathtt{r}),(t_{2},\mathtt{r})\in T

;

(5)

(7)

then there exists

,

,

R\mathrel{\hbox to35.32pt{\vbox to11.48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.65955pt\lower-7.14023pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.61 -6.28 L 15.86 -6.28 L 14.38 -7.94 L 11.43 -4.62 L 8.47 -7.94 L 5.52 -4.62 L 2.57 -7.94 L -0.38 -4.62 L -3.34 -7.94 L -6.29 -4.62 L -9.24 -7.94 L -12.19 -4.62 L -15.15 -7.94 L -18.1 -4.62 L -21.05 -7.94 L -24.16 -6.28 L -24.16 -6.28}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{17.25955pt}{-4.54025pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

and

(R^{\prime},\langle S,T,F,\mathsf{m}^{\prime}\rangle)\in\mathcal{R}
.

The largest forward reverse bisimulation is called forward reverse
bisimilarity, denoted with
\sim_{FR}
.

We first prove that two coherent RCCS processes which are structurally congruent are encoded within the

same

rUN
. Subsequently, we demonstrate the equivalence between a
step taken in the process algebra and the firing of an appropriate transition
in the corresponding network, and vice versa.

Lemma 11

(Preservation)
.

Let
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
be two coherent RCCS processes. If
R_{1}\equiv R_{2}
then

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 3.0mu$}}

and

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 3.0mu$}}

are isomorphic and have the same marking
up to places renaming.

Proof 4.4
.

Since
\equiv
is defined on monitored processes, then the only axiom which
changes the structure of the ancestor process is the
\alpha
-renaming.
Hence
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
have the same ancestor, say
P
, up to

\alpha
-renaming. It is easy to see that the two generated nets have the
same places, transitions and flow relation up to renaming, hence they are
isomorphic.
We just have to check whether the initial markings are the same. The proof
follows by induction and case analysis on the last applied axiom of

\equiv
:

Split:

If the last applied rule is (
Split
), w.l.o.g. we can
assume
R_{1}=m\triangleright(P_{1}\parallel P_{2})
and

R_{2}=\langle 0\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P_{1}\parallel\langle 1\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P_{2}
. We need to show
that
\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})=\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})
. By looking at the definition of

\mathbf{\mu}({\cdot})
we have that

\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})=\mathsf{m}\text{ and }

and

\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})=\mathbf{\mu}({\langle 0\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P_{1}})\bowtie\mathbf{\mu}({\langle 1\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P_{2}})

Also, by definition of
\mathbf{\mu}({\cdot})
we have that
\mathsf{m}=\mathsf{m}_{0}\cup\mathtt{path}(m)\mathsf{m}_{P_{1}\parallel P_{2}}
, where
\mathsf{m}_{P_{1}\parallel P_{2}}
is the initial marking of the net encoding
(P_{1}\parallel P_{2})
.
Hence, we can divide this marking into the marking of
P_{1}
and the marking of
P_{2}
as follows:

\mathsf{m}=\mathsf{m}_{0}\cup\mathtt{path}(m){\sf\|_{0}}\mathsf{m}_{P_{1}}\cup\mathtt{path}(m){\sf\|_{1}}\mathsf{m}_{P_{2}}

Also, we have that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({\langle 0\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P_{1}})=\mathsf{m}_{a}\cup\mathtt{path}(\langle 0\rangle\cdot{m})\mathsf{m}_{P_{1}}=\mathsf{m}^{a}_{0}\cup\mathtt{path}(m){\sf\|_{0}}\mathsf{m}_{P_{1}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({\langle 1\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P_{2}})=\mathsf{m}_{b}\cup\mathtt{path}(\langle 1\rangle\cdot{m})\mathsf{m}_{P_{2}}=\mathsf{m}^{b}_{0}\cup\mathtt{path}(m){\sf\|_{1}}\mathsf{m}_{P_{2}}

Were
\mathsf{m}_{a}=\mathsf{m}_{b}=\mathsf{m}_{0}
, since it is the marking
derived from the information contained into the memory
m
. Now, it is simple
to conclude, since
\mathsf{m}_{a}\bowtie\mathsf{m}_{b}=\mathsf{m}_{0}\bowtie\mathsf{m}_{0}=\mathsf{m}_{0}
, as they are the same
marking and since we are considering reachable processes it is impossible for a process to synchronise in the future with itself, hence
\bowtie
acts as the normal set union.

Res:

this case is a simplified version of the previous one.

\alpha
:

Suppose
\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})=\mathsf{m}\cup\mathsf{m^{\prime}}
where

\mathsf{m^{\prime}}
is the markings containing the bound action which will be
converted by the last application of
\equiv
. By inductive hypothesis we
also have that
\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})=\mathsf{m}\cup\alpha(\mathsf{m^{\prime}})
where the

\alpha
-conversion is applied only to those names which contains the
bound action, that is
\mathsf{m^{\prime}}
. We have that the two
nets have the same marking up to some renaming, as desired.

Lemma 12

(Soundness)
.

Let
R_{1}
be an RCCS coherent process and

\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}\!=\!\langle S,T,F,\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})\rangle
its
corresponding
rUN
. If
R_{1}\xhookrightarrow{\hat{m}:\alpha}R_{2}
then

•

\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}={\langle S,T,F,\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})\rangle}
; and

•

there exists
t\in T
such that
\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})\,[\/{t}\/\rangle\,\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})
; and

•

for some
d\in\{\mathtt{f},\mathtt{r}\}
either

–

\hat{m}=m
and
t=(\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha,d)
; or

–

\hat{m}=m_{1},m_{2}
and
\alpha=\tau
and there exist two
transitions
(t_{1},d),(t_{2},d)\in T
with
\overline{\ell(t_{1})}=\ell(t_{2})
and

\mathtt{path}(m_{i})\alpha_{i}=t_{i}
with
\ell(t_{i})=\alpha_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, and
t=(\{t_{1},t_{2}\},d)
.

Proof 4.5
.

As
R_{1}
is a coherent process then it has an ancestor
{\rho}(R_{1})
, say

P
which is unique (thanks to
8
), which is the same ancestor of
R_{2}
, as
R_{2}
is reached by
R_{1}
with
one reduction step. Therefore

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 3.0mu$}}

and

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 3.0mu$}}

have the same places, transitions and flow
relation, the only difference being the marking. We show that for each move in
the process algebra a corresponding firing of a transition
t\in T
exists such
that
(\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})\setminus{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t})\cup{t}{{}^{\bullet}}=\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})
.

We have
two cases: either the process synchronises with the context or it performs a

\tau
(or a reversing of any of them). Both cases are similar, so we will
focus on the first one.
We proceed by induction on the derivation
R_{1}\xrightarrow{{m}:{\alpha}}R_{2}
with a
case analysis on the last applied rule. The base cases correspond to the
application of either
r-act
or
\textsc{r-act}^{\bullet}
.

r-act:

Consider the application of the rule
r-act
. We have

R_{1}=m\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}\xrightarrow{{m}:{\alpha_{z}}}\langle{*},{\alpha^{z}_{z}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{z\}}\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P_{z}=R_{2}

We first consider the case where
|I|=1
. Hence we have

R_{1}=m\triangleright\alpha.Q\xrightarrow{{m}:{\alpha}}\langle{*},{\alpha},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright Q=R_{2}

The marking corresponding to
R_{1}
in the net
\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}={\langle S,T,F,\mathsf{m}\rangle}
is

\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})=\mathbf{\mu}({m\triangleright\alpha.Q})

and thanks to
9
the marking of
R_{2}
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{\alpha},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright Q})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\mu}({m\triangleright\alpha.Q})\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha.Q\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m)\hat{\alpha}.\underline{\alpha}\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m)\hat{\alpha}.\mathsf{m}_{Q}\}

By construction (see
Section

4.1
), the net

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}

contains a transition
t\in T
such that
t=(\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha,\mathtt{f})
, with

{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t}=\{\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha.Q\}
and

{t}{{}^{\bullet}}=\{\mathtt{path}(m)\hat{\alpha}.\underline{\alpha}\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m)\hat{\alpha}.\mathsf{m}_{Q}\}
.
The thesis follows by observing that such transition is enabled at
\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})
because

\{\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha.Q\}\in\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})
by
definition of
\mathbf{\mu}({\cdot})
, and
\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})\,[\/{t}\/\rangle\,\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})
.

Consider now the case with
|I|>1
.

R_{1}=m\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}\xrightarrow{{m}:{\alpha_{z}}}\langle{*},{\alpha^{z}_{z}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{z\}}\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright P_{z}=R_{2}

The marking corresponding to
R_{1}
in the net
\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}={\langle S,T,F,\mathsf{m}\rangle}
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\mu}({m\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathbf{\mu}({m\triangleright\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}})

and it contains the marked places
\{{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{i}}\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}}\mid{i\in I}\}
.
Again by construction, the net

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}

contains a transition
t\in T
such that
t=(\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\alpha_{z},\mathtt{f})
, with
z\in I
,

{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t}=\{{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{i}}\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}}\mid{i\in I}\}
and

{t}{{}^{\bullet}}=\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\underline{\alpha_{z}}\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\mathsf{m}_{Q_{z}}\}
and again

\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})\,[\/{t}\/\rangle\,\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})
where

\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})
is the marking

\qquad\quad\;\mathbf{\mu}({m\triangleright\!\sum_{i\in I}\!\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}})\!\setminus\!\{{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{i}}\alpha_{i}.Q_{i}\!}\mid{\!i\!\in\!I}\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\underline{\alpha_{z}}\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{z}}\hat{\alpha}_{z}.\mathsf{m}_{Q_{z}}\}

r-act
∙
:

The case in
which (
r-act
∙
) is used is similar.
Assume

and again take
|I|\!=\!1
.​Then

\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})\!=\!\mathbf{\mu}({\langle{*},{\alpha},{\mathbf{0}}\rangle\cdot{\!}{m}\!\triangleright\!Q})\!=\!\mathbf{\mu}({m\triangleright\alpha.Q})\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha.Q\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m)\hat{\alpha}.\underline{\alpha}\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}(m)\hat{\alpha}.\mathsf{m}_{Q}\}
. The transition

t=(\mathtt{path}(m)a,\mathtt{r})
in

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}

is enabled at

\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})
as it is the reverse of
(\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha,\mathtt{f})
and its execution
leads to the marking
\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})=\mathbf{\mu}({m\triangleright\alpha.Q})
as required.

The case with
|I|>1
follows the same argument of the forward one.

In the inductive case we have to do a case analysis on the last applied
rule. We have (
l-par
), (
r-sych
), (
r-res
) and
(
r-equiv
) and their reversible variants. The most representative
cases are (
r-sych
) and (
r-equiv
).

r-equiv:

Consider the application of the rule (
r-equiv
). It follows by induction and by applying
11
.

r-equiv
∙
:

The application of the rule (
r-equiv
∙
) follows the same argument of the previous case.

r-synch:

For the (
r-sych
)
case, let us suppose
R_{0}=R^{1}_{0}\parallel R^{2}_{0}
. We have that

R^{1}_{0}\parallel R^{2}_{0}\xrightarrow{{m_{1},m_{2}}:{\tau}}R^{1}_{1_{m_{1}@m_{2}}}\parallel R^{2}_{1_{m_{2}@m_{1}}}
with
R^{i}_{0}\xrightarrow{{m_{i}}:{\alpha_{i}}}R^{i}_{1}
and

\alpha_{1}=\overline{\alpha_{2}}
. By applying the inductive hypothesis on the
derivations
R^{i}_{0}\xrightarrow{{m_{i}}:{\alpha_{i}}}R^{i}_{1}
we have that there exists two
transitions
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
such that

\mathsf{m^{i}_{r_{0}}}\,[\/{t_{i}}\/\rangle\,\mathsf{m_{r_{1}}^{i}}
,

(\mathtt{path}(m_{i})\alpha_{i},\mathtt{f})=t_{i}
,
\mathbf{\mu}({R^{i}_{0}})=\mathsf{m^{i}_{r_{0}}}
and

\mathbf{\mu}({R^{i}_{1}})=\mathsf{m^{i}_{r_{1}}}
. We can desume that

{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t_{1}}\cap{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t_{2}}=\emptyset
, since they are enabled on different
markings. Also, by definition we have that

\mathsf{m_{r_{0}}}=\mathbf{\mu}({R_{0}})=\mathbf{\mu}({R^{1}_{0}})\bowtie\mathbf{\mu}({R^{2}_{0}})
. Let us note
that the operator
\bowtie
acts on places which corresponds to past
synchronisations, hence it does not affect
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t_{i}}
, that is

{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t_{i}}\in\mathsf{m_{r_{0}}}
. Since
\alpha_{1}=\overline{\alpha_{2}}
then by

Section

4.1
in the net there exists a transition

t_{\tau}=(\{\mathtt{path}(m_{1})\alpha_{1},\mathtt{path}(m_{2})\alpha_{2}\},\mathtt{f})
where the preset
and postset are respectively

{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t_{\tau}}={\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t_{1}}\cup{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t_{2}}
and

{t_{\tau}}{{}^{\bullet}}=({t_{1}}{{}^{\bullet}}\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m_{1})\hat{\alpha}_{1}.\underline{\alpha_{1}}\})\cup({t_{2}}{{}^{\bullet}}\setminus\{\mathtt{path}(m_{2})\hat{\alpha}_{2}.\underline{\alpha_{2}}\})\cup\{s_{\{\mathtt{path}(m_{1})\alpha_{1},\mathtt{path}(m_{2})\alpha_{2}\}}\}
.
Hence we have that

\mathsf{m_{r_{0}}}\,[\/{t_{\tau}}\/\rangle\,(\mathsf{m_{r}}\setminus{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t_{\tau}})\cup{t_{\tau}}{{}^{\bullet}}
. By definition we have that

\{\mathtt{path}(m_{1})\alpha_{1},m_{2}\}\in\mathbf{\mu}({R^{1}_{1}})
and

\{\mathtt{path}(m_{2})\alpha_{2},m_{1}\}\in\mathbf{\mu}({R^{2}_{1}})
and that

\{\mathtt{path}(m_{1})\alpha_{1},\mathtt{path}(m_{2})\alpha_{2}\}\in\mathbf{\mu}({R^{1}_{1}})\bowtie\mathbf{\mu}({R^{2}_{1}})
. Also let us note that the
m_{i}@m_{j}
operation
just replace the
*
on top of the memory
m_{i}
with
m_{j}
, which is similar
to the
\bowtie
operator. Hence

\mathbf{\mu}({R^{1}_{1}})\bowtie\mathbf{\mu}({R^{2}_{1}})=\mathbf{\mu}({R^{1}_{1_{m_{1}@m_{2}}}\parallel R^{2}_{1_{m_{2}@m_{1}}}})=\mathsf{m_{r_{2}}}
, as desired.

r-sych
∙
:

this case is analogous to (
\textsc{r-act}^{\bullet}
).

Lemma 13

(Completeness)
.

Let
R_{1}
be an RCCS coherent process and let

\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{1})\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}=\langle S{},T{},F{},{\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})}\rangle
be the
corresponding
rUN
. If
\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})\,[\/{t}\/\rangle\,\mathsf{m}^{\prime}
, then there exists
R_{2}
s.t.
one of the following holds:

•

t=(\mathtt{path}(m)\alpha,d)
and
R_{1}\xhookrightarrow{m:\alpha}R_{2}
and

\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}=\langle S{},T{},F{},{\mathsf{m}^{\prime}}\rangle
;

•

t=(\{t_{1},t_{2}\},d)
such that
\overline{\ell(t_{1})}=\ell(t_{2})
, with

t_{i}=(\mathtt{path}(m_{i})\alpha_{i},d)
,

\alpha_{i}\in\{\ell(t_{1}),\ell(t_{2})\}
for
i=1,2
and

R_{1}\xhookrightarrow{m_{1},m_{2}:\tau}R_{2}
with

\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}(R_{2})\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}=\langle S{},T{},F{},{\mathsf{m}^{\prime}}\rangle

with
d\in\{\mathtt{f},\mathtt{r}\}
.

Proof 4.6
.

If
\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})\,[\/{t}\/\rangle\,\mathsf{m}^{\prime}
, then

\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})=\mathsf{m}_{0}\cup{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t}
and

\mathsf{m}^{\prime}=(\mathsf{m}_{0}\setminus{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t})\cup{t}{{}^{\bullet}}
. The encoding
of
\mathcal{N}(\cdot)
is such that each transition or place name has a unique
form, which corresponds to a path of a CCS term, and the transitions in

\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(\cdot)\mkern 3.0mu$}}

are of the form
(t,d)
, where
t
is the
transition name of the CCS term and
d\in\{\mathtt{f},\mathtt{r}\}
is the

direction
, either forward or reverse. That is from the transition
name
(t,d)
we can isolate the RCCS term which can mimic the action.

If the transition
(t,d)
is not a synchronisation, that is
(t,d)
is not of
the form
(\{t_{1},t_{2}\},d)
, then we can assume w.l.o.g. that

t=(\tilde{\phi}\alpha,d)
with
\tilde{\phi}
being a sequence of

\phi\in\{{\sf\|_{i}},{\sf+_{i}},\hat{\alpha},{\sf\setminus_{a}}\}
. Suppose

d
is
\mathtt{f}
. If the last decoration in
\tilde{\phi}
has the form

{\sf+_{j}}
, that is
\tilde{\phi}=\tilde{\phi}^{\prime}{\sf+_{j}}
this means
there exists in the net a set of transition

T^{\prime}=\{t_{i}=(\tilde{\phi}\beta_{i},\mathtt{f})\,\mid\,(t_{i},\mathtt{f})\in T\}
. Now, let
assume that the ancestor of
R_{1}
is
P
, we have that

P=C[\sum_{i\in I}\beta_{i}.Q_{i}]
where there exists an index

j\in I
such that
\beta_{j}=\alpha
and
\alpha
is the action mimicked by
the transition
(t,\mathtt{f})
and the right position of the hole in the context
is calculated using
\tilde{\phi}
.
Also, since the transition is enabled in the net, then also

R_{1}=E[(m\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\beta_{i}.Q_{i})\backslash{A}]
where

E[\cdot]
is an active context. Hence, we have that

E[(m\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\beta_{i}.Q_{i})\backslash{A}]\xrightarrow{{m}:{\beta^{j}}}E[(\langle{*},{\beta^{j}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{j\}}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright Q_{j})\backslash{A}]=R_{2}

By definition
4.2
we have

\mathsf{m}=\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})
, and by definition
4.1

{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t}=\{\tilde{\phi}\beta_{i}.Q_{i}\,\mid\,i\in I\}
and

{t}{{}^{\bullet}}=\{\tilde{\phi}\hat{\beta}_{j}.\underline{\beta_{j}}\}\cup\tilde{\phi}.\{\beta_{j}.Q_{j}\}
. Also

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\mu}({E[\mathbf{0}]})\bowtie\mathbf{\mu}({(m\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\beta_{i}.Q_{i})\backslash{A}})=\mathsf{m}\cup\mathsf{m}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\mu}({E[\mathbf{0}]})\bowtie\mathbf{\mu}({(\langle{*},{\beta^{j}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{j\}}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright Q_{j})\backslash{A}})=\mathsf{m}\cup\mathsf{m}_{2}

where
\mathsf{m_{1}}
and
\mathsf{m_{2}}
are the results of applying the
eventual synchronisation
\bowtie
respectively on

\mathbf{\mu}({(m\triangleright\sum_{i\in I}\beta_{i}.Q_{i})\backslash{A}})
and

\mathbf{\mu}({(\langle{*},{\beta^{j}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{j\}}}\rangle\cdot{m}\triangleright Q_{j})\backslash{A}})
. Moreover, we can separate from
\mathsf{m}_{1}
and

\mathsf{m}_{2}
the key places, that is the places whose name terminates with

\hat{\alpha}.\underline{\alpha}
or with
s_{\{t_{1},t_{2}\}}
. Be

\mathsf{m}^{k}_{i}
such markings then we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R_{1}})=\mathsf{m}\cup\mathsf{m}_{1}=\mathsf{m}\cup\mathsf{m}^{k}_{1}\cup\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathbf{\mu}({R_{2}})=\mathsf{m}\cup\mathsf{m}_{2}=\mathsf{m}\cup\mathsf{m}^{k}_{2}\cup\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{2}

By definition of
\mathbf{\mu}({\cdot})
we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{1}=\{\mathtt{path}(m).{\sf+_{i}}\beta_{i}.Q_{i}\,\mid\,i\in I\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{m}^{\prime}_{2}=\{\mathtt{path}((\langle{*},{\beta^{j}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{j\}}}\rangle\cdot{m}).\hat{\beta}_{j}.\underline{\beta_{j}}\}\cup\{\mathtt{path}((\langle{*},{\beta^{j}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{j\}}}\rangle\cdot{m}).\hat{\beta}_{j}.Q_{j}\}

It is easy to check that
\tilde{\phi}=\mathtt{path}(m){\sf+_{i}}
and

\tilde{\phi}=\mathtt{path}((\langle{*},{\beta^{j}},{\sum_{i\in I\setminus\{j\}}}\rangle\cdot{m})
. And we are done.

The cases of synchonisation and backward transitions are similar.

We can now state our main result in terms of bisimulation:

Theorem 14
.

Let
R
be an RCCS process and let
P={\rho}(R)
be its
ancestor, then

\langle\rangle\triangleright P\sim_{FR}\mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}

Proof 4.7
.

It is sufficient to show that

\mathcal{R}=\{(R,\langle S,T,F,\mathbf{\mu}({R})\rangle)\ |\ {\rho}(R)=P,\ \mathchoice{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 3.0mu\reflectbox{$\textstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-3.0mu\textstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 3.0mu$}}$}\mkern-3.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}{\mkern 2.0mu\reflectbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\overrightarrow{\reflectbox{$\mkern-2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}(P)\mkern 2.0mu$}}$}\mkern-2.0mu}=\langle S,T,F,\mathsf{m}\rangle\}

is a
forward and reverse bisimulation. It is easy to check that all the conditions of
Definition
4.3
are matched by Lemmas
12
and
13
.

5.
Conclusions and future works

On the line of previous research we have equipped a reversible process
calculus with a non sequential semantics by using one of the classical
encoding of process calculi into nets.
What comes out from the encoding is that the machinery to reverse a process
was already present in the encoding.
Other approaches to address true concurrency in reversible calculi have been
explored, for instance
[
Aub22
,
Aub24
]
, where a proved semantics
[
DP92
]

for CCSK is given. This requires to revisit the LTS of CCSK in order to add
extra information in the labels, about the process which contributed to an
action, and then to derive a true-concurrent notion. Our approach directly
compiles RCCS into a truly concurrent model, and hence we do not need to
modify the lts of RCCS. Hence we exploit the natively truly concurrent
semantics of Petri nets in order to retrieve a truly concurrent semantics of
RCCS. Also our approach accounts for infinite behaviours, while

[
Aub22
,
Aub24
]
do not.

The current results applies to RCCS, but we do believe that the same encoding
could be used to model CCSK processes. As a matter of fact, in CCSK the
information is stored directly in the process and executed prefixes are marked
with communications keys and in our encoding it is signalled by a token in

key
-places. For example if we take the process
P=a.Q
in CCSK the
process evolves in
a[i].Q
where the forward behaviour of the process is
Q

while the backward behaviour is represented by the marked prefix
a[i]
. The
same mechanisms applies to synchronisations. If we take the process

a.b.\mathbf{0}\parallel\overline{a}.\mathbf{0}
the process can make a synchronisation
followed by the
b
action and evolves to
a[i].b[j].\mathbf{0}\parallel\overline{a}[i].\mathbf{0}
. In this way, the synchronisation on
a
cannot be undone if
first the action
b
is undone. By looking on how history information is kept
into CCSK processes, it is clear that there is a tight correspondence between
the marked prefixes, the key-places and
\hat{\cdot}
decorations we have
used in unravel nets. Also in CCSK the process structure does not change, and
the marking of the reversible net would correspond to the marked prefixes.
This seems to bring a more straightforward encoding of CCSK into Petri Nets,
where the marking can be easily retrieved from a CCSK term. Having the two encodings into Petri Net would
allow us for cross-fertilization results, in line with
[
LMM21
]
.
The
whole encoding and the machinery connected to it is left for future work.

Our result relies on unravel nets, that are able to represent

or
-causality. The consequence is that the same event may have different
pasts. Unravel nets are naturally related to
bundle
event structures

[
Lan92
,
LBK97
]
, where the dependencies are represented using

bundles
, namely finite subsets of conflicting events, and the bundle
relation is usually written as
X\mapsto e
.
Starting from an unravel net
\langle S{},T{},F{},\mathsf{m}{}\rangle
, and considering the transition

t\in T
, the bundles representing the dependencies are
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{s}\mapsto t
for
each
s\in{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{t}
, and the conflict relation can be easily inferred by the
semantic one definable on the unravel net. This result relies on the fact that
in any unravel net, for each place
s
, the transitions in
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{s}
are
pairwise conflicting. The
reversible
bundle structures add to the
bundle relation (defined also on the reversing events) a prevention relation,
and the intuition behind this relation is the usual one: some events, possibly
depending on the one to be reversed, are still present and they
prevent

that event to be reversed. The problem here is that in an unravel net,
differently from occurrence nets, is not so easy to determine which
transitions depend on the happening of a specific one, thus potentially
preventing it from being reversed. An idea would be to consider all the
transitions in
{s}{{}^{\bullet}}
for each
s\in{t}{{}^{\bullet}}
, but it has to be carefully
checked if this is enough. Thus, which is the proper “reversible bundle event
structure” corresponding to the reversible unravel nets has to be answered,
though it is likely that the conditions to be posed on the prevention
relations will be similar to the ones considered in

[
GPY18
,
GPY21
]
. Once that also this step is done, we
will have the full correspondence between reversible processes calculi and non
sequential models.

Another future works idea would be to move from reversible CCS to reversible

\pi
-calculus
[
LMS16
,
CKV13
]
by relying on the results of

[
BG09
]
. In
[
BG09
]
a truly concurrent semantics of

\pi
-calculus is given in form of Petri nets with inhibitor arcs. We could
exploit our previous results on reversibility and Petri nets with inhibitor
arcs
[
MMP21a
,
MMP23
,
MMP24
]
to obtain a truly concurrent semantics
for reversible
\pi
-calculus in Petri nets with inhibitor arcs. Alternatively
we could exploit the encoding of reversible
\pi
-calculus into rigid families
(based on configuration structures), given in
[
CKV16
]
, and
bring it to Petri nets.

Appendix A
Implementation

We describe an effective implementation of the proposed
encoding in
Haskell

2
2

2

The code can be accessed at
https://github.com/hmelgra/reversible-ccs-as-nets
.
.
The intent of this section is to provide a practical evidence that the coinductive approach to describe infinite behaviours
is effective, rather than providing a fully fledged tool. We are aware there exist other Petri net implementations in Haskel (see for example
[
Rei99
]
), but a comparison with such tools is out of the scope of this section.

A.1.
Representation of infinite nets

When working with an infinite data structure, a pivotal aspect is devising an
efficient strategy to traverse the pertinent section of the structure. In our
specific scenario, we prioritise the capability to identify and execute
enabled transitions within a (potentially infinite) net.
Therefore, our main objective is to identify those transitions that are
enabled at a given marking. For this purpose, we adopt a representation of infinite nets that facilitates
obtaining a truncated version of the net that contains all the enabled
transitions in given marking.
To maintain simplicity, we avoid explicitly representing the flow relation as
a set of pairs. Instead, we associate each transition with its preset (input
places) and postset (output places). Consequently, we rely on the following
instrumental datatype to represent transitions.

⬇

The components of a net include a marking, denoted as
netMarking
, which
is essentially a set of places. Additionally, there are two functions,

netPlaces
and
netTransitions
, which map every marking to a set of
places and transitions, respectively, of a truncated, finite version of the
net. This truncated net includes all the transitions from the potentially
infinite net that are enabled in the given marking.

{exa}

Consider the infinite net
N
depicted in Figure
16A
.
One potential
Haskell
definition for
N
could be
nAt

[0]
, utilising
the function
nAt
given in Figure
16B
. This function takes
a marking of type
[
Int
]
and returns a net with place names represented
as integers and transitions as strings, i.e., of type
Net

Int

String
.
The net’s definition relies on the functions
p
and
t
, which
determine the truncation of the net corresponding to a given marking. It is
important to note that, for a specific marking
m
, any enabled
transition
t
in
m
should satisfy the conditions
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}t=\{i-1\}

and
{t}{{}^{\bullet}}=\{i\}
, where
0<i\leq m
, and
m
is the maximum integer in

m
. Therefore, the function
p
, which maps markings to sets of
places, is defined as follows:

•

For the empty marking, it returns an empty set of places since no
transitions are enabled in the empty marking.

•

For a non-empty marking
m
, it generates a list containing all
integers in the range from
0
to the maximum value in
m
plus one.

Similarly, the function
t
creates a list of transitions, encompassing
all those among the places in
p m
. These transitions are defined in
such a way that
[
i

-

1]
represents its preset, and
[
i
]

represents its postset. The name of the
i
-th transition is denoted by
i

occurrences of
’a’
.

0
1
2
a
aa
…

A

N

B

Haskell
code

⬇

The guard
isTransition

t

n
simply checks that
t
appears in the
truncation of the net
n
. In such case, a transition
t
is enabled if
all elements in its preset appear in the marking of the net. Otherwise, the
transition is not enabled.

The firing of a transition straightforwardly changes the marking of the net
as expected, i.e., by removing the preset of the transition and by adding the
postset of the transition

We highlight that the datatype
CCS
includes constructors for the finite
definition of infinite processes, i.e.,
Var
for a process variable and

Rec
for a recursive definition.
This choice is down to the facts that (i) our encoding uses CCS processes as
the names of the elements of the generated nets; and (ii) the operational
semantics of nets is defined under the assumption that names can be
effectively compared (see details below). In order to have an equality test
for infinite terms, we opted for a finite representation.
Hence, the infinite CCS process consisting of an infinite sequence
of inputs over the channel
a
can be defined as follows

⬇

This new type aims to enhance the parsing of strings into

This new type has been introduced in order to facilitate the parsing of

Read
.

The function
unfold
will be used in the definition of the encoding.

Despite we rely on the finite representation of CCS processes, we remark
that the implementation of the encoding associates
infinite
nets to
recursive CCS processes.

A.3.
Implementation of the encoding

According to the encoding introduced in
Section

4
, the names of the places
and transitions of the obtained nets are (possibly) decorated CCS processes.
We rely on the following datatypes introducing constructors for the names of
places and transitions.

⬇

The above definitions are in one-to-one correspondence with the names introduced
by the encoding of the previous Section, and self-explanatory.

We will use the predicate
isKey
on place’s names that determines if a
place name is a key, i.e., either
PKey
or
PSync
(its omitted
definition is straightforward).

The fact that the net is defined in terms of the constant functions

const

[
Proc

Nil
]
and
const

[]
reflect that every finite truncation,
independently from the given marking, consists of just one place
Proc

Nil

and none transition. The marking
[
Proc

Nil
]
assigns one token to the
unique place.

The encoding of a prefixed process follows
Section

4.1
. Hence, the
encoding of
\alpha.P
(written
a

:.
p
in the implementation) is built on
top of the encoding of
P
, i.e., the names of the places and the transitions
appearing in the encoding of
P
are decorated with the prefix

\hat{\alpha}
. We use
PPref

a
for decorating a place name with the
past of action
a
and similarly
TPref

a
for a transition name.
The following function (whose defining equations are omitted because are
uninteresting) is in charge of applying renamings to a net.

⬇

A.4.

Consider the network, denoted as
Net

s

t

m
, which is translated into a new net with the same sets of places and markings, represented as

s

t
’

m
. The set of transitions
t
’

x

y

z
from the original set

•

Transition

(
Fwd

x
)

y

z
, to tag
each transition in
t

•

Add the corresponding reversing transition, denoted as

Transition

(
Rev

x
)

y

z
, to maintain the bidirectional nature of the net.

This process is defined as the parallel composition of two infinite
processes, where the shared name

To obtain the corresponding reversible net, we apply the encoding

(

compute the firing of transitions, we can seamlessly implement a
simulation function to replicate the behavior of the process.

shows the set of enabled transitions of the obtained net, which are as follows.

⬇

1)

\rightarrow\
(
\mid
r
:
+
l
:2!)\1

2)

\rightarrow\
(
\mid
l
:1?
*
\mid
r
:
+
r
:1!)\1

The name
(
\mid
r
:
+
l
:2!)\1
of the first transition indicates that it
corresponds to the output performed on channel
2
by the left branch
(i.e.,
+
l
:
) of the right hand of the parallel composition (i.e.,

\mid
r
:
). Similarly, the symbol
*
in the name
\mid
l
:1?
*
\mid
r
:
+
r
:1!)\1

indicates that the transition corresponds to a synchronisation between the
input performed on channel
1
by the left hand side of the parallel
composition (i.e.,
\mid
l
:
) and the output on channel
1
performed by the
right branch of the right hand side of the parallel composition (i.e.,

\mid
r
:
+
r
:
).

At this point, any of the two transitions can be fired. After firing the first one,
the obtained set of enabled transitions is the following.

⬇

1)

\rightarrow\
(
\mid
r
:
+
l
:^2!.
+
l
:2!)\1

2)

\rightarrow\
(
\mid
l
:1?
*
\mid
r
:
+
l
:^2!.
+
r
:1!)\1

3)

<
-(
\mid
r
:
+
l
:2!)\1

The first two transitions mirror the ones originally enabled; however, their names indicate that actions on the right-hand side of the parallel composition causally depend on the preceding performed action (the prefix
+
l
:^2!
).

In addition to these two forward transitions, there is one reversing transition that undoes the previously executed action.
